From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Well, what brought me in is that... some evenings after work, especially when I've been really strict all day, I just end up eating way more than I meant to. Like, I'll eat dinner, and then... then I'll have cereal, crackers, whatever leftovers are around, and sweets, all within just a couple of hours. It's just... it's been bothering me, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like... once I get started on those nights, I just keep going back to the kitchen. Even while I'm in my head telling myself to stop, I just keep eating. I don't really feel back in charge of things until I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I feel really guilty about it. It's just frustrating because... well, I know better, you know? I should be able to control myself. So, yeah, I definitely feel pretty ashamed once it's all over and I realize how much I actually just ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm rushing or eating super fast. It's more just... a steady pace of eating that goes on for a while. I'm just constantly eating over those couple of hours, rather than wolfing it all down in a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, I'd say it happens most weeks. It's usually about one or two nights a week where I end up eating far more than I planned to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll usually skip meals, or I'll spend a lot more time exercising just to try and fix what I did the night before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. Um... I just try to handle it by skipping meals the next day or doing extra exercise, like I mentioned. But no, no vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My weight has stayed pretty steady, honestly. I mean, maybe it fluctuates a pound or two up or down depending on the week, but I haven't noticed any significant changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try and make up for the extra food you ate during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, just what I mentioned before. I just stick to skipping meals or doing that extra workout. I don't take anything or do anything else to try and fix it... just the fasting and the exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you skip meals or exercise more after these episodes is to try not to gain weight from all the extra food you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's why I do it. The next day after one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it so I don't gain weight. I'll skip breakfast and lunch, or I'll go out for an extra-long hike... or I'll push myself a lot harder at softball conditioning, even when I'm already really tired. I just feel like I have to balance it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still try to make up for those extra calories through exercise or skipping meals if you didn't think it would help you avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... if I knew it wouldn't make a difference with my weight, I probably wouldn't push myself so hard. The whole point of skipping the meals or doing the extra workouts is just to make sure I don't gain anything from what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to eat in this way if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, the whole reason I eat like that—the binging, I guess—it's usually because I've been restricting myself so much trying to keep my weight down. If I didn't care about my weight, I probably wouldn't restrict in the first place, and then I wouldn't end up losing control like that. So, no, I don't think I would.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, what brought me in is that... some evenings after work, especially when I've been really strict all day, I just end up eating way more than I meant to. Like, I'll eat dinner, and then... then I'll have cereal, crackers, whatever leftovers are around, and sweets, all within just a couple of hours. It's just... it's been bothering me, so I figured I should talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during those episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. It's like... once I get started on those nights, I just keep going back to the kitchen. Even while I'm in my head telling myself to stop, I just keep eating. I don't really feel back in charge of things until I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I feel really guilty about it. It's just frustrating because... well, I know better, you know? I should be able to control myself. So, yeah, I definitely feel pretty ashamed once it's all over and I realize how much I actually just ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm rushing or eating super fast. It's more just... a steady pace of eating that goes on for a while. I'm just constantly eating over those couple of hours, rather than wolfing it all down in a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, I'd say it happens most weeks. It's usually about one or two nights a week where I end up eating far more than I planned to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll usually skip meals, or I'll spend a lot more time exercising just to try and fix what I did the night before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. Um... I just try to handle it by skipping meals the next day or doing extra exercise, like I mentioned. But no, no vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My weight has stayed pretty steady, honestly. I mean, maybe it fluctuates a pound or two up or down depending on the week, but I haven't noticed any significant changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try and make up for the extra food you ate during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, just what I mentioned before. I just stick to skipping meals or doing that extra workout. I don't take anything or do anything else to try and fix it... just the fasting and the exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you skip meals or exercise more after these episodes is to try not to gain weight from all the extra food you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's why I do it. The next day after one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it so I don't gain weight. I'll skip breakfast and lunch, or I'll go out for an extra-long hike... or I'll push myself a lot harder at softball conditioning, even when I'm already really tired. I just feel like I have to balance it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still try to make up for those extra calories through exercise or skipping meals if you didn't think it would help you avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... if I knew it wouldn't make a difference with my weight, I probably wouldn't push myself so hard. The whole point of skipping the meals or doing the extra workouts is just to make sure I don't gain anything from what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to eat in this way if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, the whole reason I eat like that—the binging, I guess—it's usually because I've been restricting myself so much trying to keep my weight down. If I didn't care about my weight, I probably wouldn't restrict in the first place, and then I wouldn't end up losing control like that. So, no, I don't think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are any other factors, besides trying to control your weight, that contribute to your episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I can't think of anything else. I mean, it really just comes down to the fact that I get so hungry from trying not to eat all day. I haven't really noticed any other reason for it besides just trying to keep my weight down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even when you're at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a fear, exactly. I mean... I definitely don't want to gain weight, but I'm not terrified of it or anything. I just prefer to keep my weight where it is, so I try to manage it. But I wouldn't say I'm constantly scared of becoming fat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your weight are excessive?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I wouldn't say they're excessive. I mean, I just try to watch what I eat and keep things in check. It feels pretty normal to me, just standard dieting, really. I know I restrict a bit too much sometimes, which causes the other issue, but the actual effort to control my weight... no, I don't think I'm going to extremes or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know I restrict a bit too much sometimes, which causes the other issue, but the actual effort to control my weight... no, I don't think I'm going to extremes or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't have your menstrual period?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I'll skip breakfast and lunch, or I'll go out for an extra-long hike... or I'll push myself a lot harder at softball conditioning, even when I'm already really tired. I just feel like I have to balance it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still try to make up for those extra calories through exercise or skipping meals if you didn't think it would help you avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... if I knew it wouldn't make a difference with my weight, I probably wouldn't push myself so hard. The whole point of skipping the meals or doing the extra workouts is just to make sure I don't gain anything from what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to eat in this way if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, the whole reason I eat like that—the binging, I guess—it's usually because I've been restricting myself so much trying to keep my weight down. If I didn't care about my weight, I probably wouldn't restrict in the first place, and then I wouldn't end up losing control like that. So, no, I don't think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are any other factors, besides trying to control your weight, that contribute to your episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I can't think of anything else. I mean, it really just comes down to the fact that I get so hungry from trying not to eat all day. I haven't really noticed any other reason for it besides just trying to keep my weight down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even when you're at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a fear, exactly. I mean... I definitely don't want to gain weight, but I'm not terrified of it or anything. I just prefer to keep my weight where it is, so I try to manage it. But I wouldn't say I'm constantly scared of becoming fat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your weight are excessive?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I wouldn't say they're excessive. I mean, I just try to watch what I eat and keep things in check. It feels pretty normal to me, just standard dieting, really. I know I restrict a bit too much sometimes, which causes the other issue, but the actual effort to control my weight... no, I don't think I'm going to extremes or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't have your menstrual period?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. My periods have always been pretty regular. I mean, obviously at my age things are starting to change a bit now, but I never had a time where they just stopped completely when I was younger or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specifically wrong with your relationship with food or eating, beyond just trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, food is just food to me. I know I have those times where I eat too much and then feel like I need to fix it, but I don't think there's some deep, complicated issue with how I view eating. It really just comes down to trying to keep my weight in check and sometimes messing up. So, no, I don't think there's anything else wrong beyond that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's usually about one or two nights a week where I end up eating far more than I planned to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. It's like... once I get started on those nights, I just keep going back to the kitchen. Even while I'm in my head telling myself to stop, I just keep eating. I don't really feel back in charge of things until I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I feel really guilty about it. It's just frustrating because... well, I know better, you know? I should be able to control myself. So, yeah, I definitely feel pretty ashamed once it's all over and I realize how much I actually just ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm rushing or eating super fast. It's more just... a steady pace of eating that goes on for a while. I'm just constantly eating over those couple of hours, rather than wolfing it all down in a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, I'd say it happens most weeks. It's usually about one or two nights a week where I end up eating far more than I planned to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll usually skip meals, or I'll spend a lot more time exercising just to try and fix what I did the night before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. Um... I just try to handle it by skipping meals the next day or doing extra exercise, like I mentioned. But no, no vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My weight has stayed pretty steady, honestly. I mean, maybe it fluctuates a pound or two up or down depending on the week, but I haven't noticed any significant changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try and make up for the extra food you ate during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, just what I mentioned before. I just stick to skipping meals or doing that extra workout. I don't take anything or do anything else to try and fix it... just the fasting and the exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you skip meals or exercise more after these episodes is to try not to gain weight from all the extra food you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's why I do it. The next day after one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it so I don't gain weight. I'll skip breakfast and lunch, or I'll go out for an extra-long hike... or I'll push myself a lot harder at softball conditioning, even when I'm already really tired. I just feel like I have to balance it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still try to make up for those extra calories through exercise or skipping meals if you didn't think it would help you avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... if I knew it wouldn't make a difference with my weight, I probably wouldn't push myself so hard. The whole point of skipping the meals or doing the extra workouts is just to make sure I don't gain anything from what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to eat in this way if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, the whole reason I eat like that—the binging, I guess—it's usually because I've been restricting myself so much trying to keep my weight down. If I didn't care about my weight, I probably wouldn't restrict in the first place, and then I wouldn't end up losing control like that. I haven't really noticed any other reason for it besides just trying to keep my weight down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even when you're at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a fear, exactly. But I wouldn't say I'm constantly scared of becoming fat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your weight are excessive?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I wouldn't say they're excessive. I know I restrict a bit too much sometimes, which causes the other issue, but the actual effort to control my weight... no, I don't think I'm going to extremes or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't have your menstrual period?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Well, what brought me in is that... some evenings after work, especially when I've been really strict all day, I just end up eating way more than I meant to. It's like... once I get started on those nights, I just keep going back to the kitchen. Even while I'm in my head telling myself to stop, I just keep eating. I don't really feel back in charge of things until I'm uncomfortably full.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating are over?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Um... I feel really guilty about it. It's just frustrating because... well, I know better, you know? I should be able to control myself. So, yeah, I definitely feel pretty ashamed once it's all over and I realize how much I actually just ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm rushing or eating super fast. It's more just... a steady pace of eating that goes on for a while. I'm just constantly eating over those couple of hours, rather than wolfing it all down in a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, I'd say it happens most weeks. It's usually about one or two nights a week where I end up eating far more than I planned to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll usually skip meals, or I'll spend a lot more time exercising just to try and fix what I did the night before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. Um... I just try to handle it by skipping meals the next day or doing extra exercise, like I mentioned. But no, no vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My weight has stayed pretty steady, honestly. I mean, maybe it fluctuates a pound or two up or down depending on the week, but I haven't noticed any significant changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try and make up for the extra food you ate during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, just what I mentioned before. I just stick to skipping meals or doing that extra workout. I don't take anything or do anything else to try and fix it... just the fasting and the exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you skip meals or exercise more after these episodes is to try not to gain weight from all the extra food you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's why I do it. The next day after one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it so I don't gain weight. I'll skip breakfast and lunch, or I'll go out for an extra-long hike... or I'll push myself a lot harder at softball conditioning, even when I'm already really tired. I just feel like I have to balance it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still try to make up for those extra calories through exercise or skipping meals if you didn't think it would help you avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... if I knew it wouldn't make a difference with my weight, I probably wouldn't push myself so hard. The whole point of skipping the meals or doing the extra workouts is just to make sure I don't gain anything from what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to eat in this way if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, the whole reason I eat like that—the binging, I guess—it's usually because I've been restricting myself so much trying to keep my weight down. If I didn't care about my weight, I probably wouldn't restrict in the first place, and then I wouldn't end up losing control like that. So, no, I don't think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there are any other factors, besides trying to control your weight, that contribute to your episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Um... no, not really. I can't think of anything else. I mean, it really just comes down to the fact that I get so hungry from trying not to eat all day. I haven't really noticed any other reason for it besides just trying to keep my weight down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even when you're at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a fear, exactly. I mean... I definitely don't want to gain weight, but I'm not terrified of it or anything. I just prefer to keep my weight where it is, so I try to manage it. But I wouldn't say I'm constantly scared of becoming fat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your weight are excessive?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I wouldn't say they're excessive. I mean, I just try to watch what I eat and keep things in check. It feels pretty normal to me, just standard dieting, really. I know I restrict a bit too much sometimes, which causes the other issue, but the actual effort to control my weight... no, I don't think I'm going to extremes or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't have your menstrual period?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. My periods have always been pretty regular. I mean, obviously at my age things are starting to change a bit now, but I never had a time where they just stopped completely when I was younger or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specifically wrong with your relationship with food or eating, beyond just trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, food is just food to me. I know I have those times where I eat too much and then feel like I need to fix it, but I don't think there's some deep, complicated issue with how I view eating. It really just comes down to trying to keep my weight in check and sometimes messing up. So, no, I don't think there's anything else wrong beyond that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits and the way you try to control your weight are causing you significant distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, not significant problems, no. I'm still keeping up with all my clients at work, and my mentoring role, plus all my responsibilities at home. Everything gets done. But... I guess I do get a little distracted by it sometimes. Like, if I have a public event or something coming up, I can feel less confident if I'm worrying about how my clothes are fitting. So it bothers me a bit then, but overall, I wouldn't say it's stopping me from living my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are significantly interfering with your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're significantly interfering. But it's not stopping me from doing my job or seeing people.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you meant to, followed by restricting or exercising to make up for it, happen at least once a week for the past three months?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say so. For the past few months, it's been happening most weeks. Usually, it's one or two nights a week where I just eat way more than I meant to. And then, you know, the next day I'll try to make up for it by skipping meals or pushing myself to exercise longer than usual. I feel really guilty about it. It's just frustrating because... well, I know better, you know? I should be able to control myself. So, yeah, I definitely feel pretty ashamed once it's all over and I realize how much I actually just ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I'm rushing or eating super fast. It's more just... a steady pace of eating that goes on for a while. I'm just constantly eating over those couple of hours, rather than wolfing it all down in a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: For the past few months, I'd say it happens most weeks. It's usually about one or two nights a week where I end up eating far more than I planned to. And then... well, the next day I always try to make up for it. I'll usually skip meals, or I'll spend a lot more time exercising just to try and fix what I did the night before.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don't do that. I've never made myself throw up. Um... I just try to handle it by skipping meals the next day or doing extra exercise, like I mentioned. But no, no vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. My weight has stayed pretty steady, honestly. I mean, maybe it fluctuates a pound or two up or down depending on the week, but I haven't noticed any significant changes.\n\nDoctor: Do you do anything else to try and make up for the extra food you ate during those episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Um, just what I mentioned before. I just stick to skipping meals or doing that extra workout. I don't take anything or do anything else to try and fix it... just the fasting and the exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the reason you skip meals or exercise more after these episodes is to try not to gain weight from all the extra food you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly. That's why I do it. The next day after one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it so I don't gain weight. I'll skip breakfast and lunch, or I'll go out for an extra-long hike... or I'll push myself a lot harder at softball conditioning, even when I'm already really tired. I just feel like I have to balance it out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you'd still try to make up for those extra calories through exercise or skipping meals if you didn't think it would help you avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know, honestly. I've never really thought about it like that. I guess... if I knew it wouldn't make a difference with my weight, I probably wouldn't push myself so hard. The whole point of skipping the meals or doing the extra workouts is just to make sure I don't gain anything from what I ate.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to eat in this way if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don't think so. I mean, the whole reason I eat like that—the binging, I guess—it's usually because I've been restricting myself so much trying to keep my weight down. I haven't really noticed any other reason for it besides just trying to keep my weight down.\n\nDoctor: Do you have a persistent fear of gaining weight or becoming fat, even when you're at a healthy weight?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it a fear, exactly. I mean... I definitely don't want to gain weight, but I'm not terrified of it or anything. I just prefer to keep my weight where it is, so I try to manage it. But I wouldn't say I'm constantly scared of becoming fat.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your weight are excessive?\n\nPatient: Um... no, I wouldn't say they're excessive. I mean, I just try to watch what I eat and keep things in check. It feels pretty normal to me, just standard dieting, really. I know I restrict a bit too much sometimes, which causes the other issue, but the actual effort to control my weight... no, I don't think I'm going to extremes or anything.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't have your menstrual period?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. My periods have always been pretty regular. I mean, obviously at my age things are starting to change a bit now, but I never had a time where they just stopped completely when I was younger or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's something specifically wrong with your relationship with food or eating, beyond just trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I wouldn't say that. Like, if I have a public event or something coming up, I can feel less confident if I'm worrying about how my clothes are fitting. So it bothers me a bit then, but overall, I wouldn't say it's stopping me from living my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are significantly interfering with your social or occupational functioning?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say they're significantly interfering.
